ParlaMint-BA_2016-05-24-0.u13435	I thank the chairman. I intend to provide some information that is important for the debate on this agenda point and a summary of all the activities that have led to today's agenda considering the proposed text of the Resolution. First of all, I would say that the activities of selecting the location for the disposal of radioactive and nuclear waste in the Republic of Croatia have lasted almost 30 years, i.e. since 1987. The debates were more locations in the Republic of Croatia, and from 1997 to '99. There are four other locations in the debate – Moslovakia gora, Trgovina gora, Psunj and Papuk. At that time, the strong resistance of the local population and the political influences from the list of possible locations removed three locations, so that only one location, i.e. the Shop Mountain, remained for the selection, and we often say that the Trade Mountain is the only politically defenceless location. The 90th Assembly of the Republic of Croatia is in '99. the year adopted the Programme of Spatial Arrangements of the Republic of Croatia, in which the Trade Mountain is designated as a place for disposal sites of radioactive and nuclear waste. After such a decision on the location of '99. the years begin all of our concerns and fears that in the foreseeable future, and as we have welcomed, we will have to fight against the intentions and these programmes that are citizens of this part of Bosnia and Herzegovina, but also of Croatia, and they are very aware of this as a threat to their health and life in these areas. Why these fears are justified, I don't think I should explain them specifically. In this regard, of course, there is a very strong resistance from local communities from Bosnia and Herzegovina and Croatia, but unfortunately there was no success in stopping the procedure, and I must conclude that there was a lack of response from then on by the official authorities and institutions of Bosnia and Herzegovina to challenge this part of the plan because the location is planned near the border of Bosnia and Herzegovina. Also in 2007, activities and campaigns at the local level against the proposed location were conducted in the process of adopting the Space Plan of the Municipality of Dvor, where the possibility of setting up a centre for the disposal of radioactive waste spent nuclear fuel sources was left. And then, unfortunately, there was no quality and concrete response from the state level. Although only then is the ratified Espoo Convention, the Convention on Environmental Impact Assessment across state borders that gives Bosnia and Herzegovina the right to engage in the process of adopting plans and programmes that could have negative cross-border influences. Bosnia and Herzegovina is now faced with a very serious, very serious plan and the intention to achieve a programme of the spatial arrangement of the Republic of Croatia, i.e. to build a landfill of radioactive waste along the border with Bosnia and Herzegovina. In 2014, the Croatian Parliament adopted the Strategy for the Management of Radioactive Waste of Used Sources and Exhausted Nuclear Fuel as a first step in establishing a national programme for implementation of the Strategy. In the course of last year, nearly a year ago, the Croatian Institute for Radiological and Nuclear Security launched a strategic study procedure for the National Programme for the Implementation of the Strategy for the Management of Radioactive Waste of Used Sources and Exhausted Nuclear Fuel. The House of Representatives and the House of Peoples adopted a set of conclusions that follow the procedure and require the competent authorities and institutions of Bosnia and Herzegovina to challenge the possibility of building a landfill of radioactive waste in the Shop Gora, i.e. the microlocation of Cerkezovac, which is only 850 metres away from the river Una and the city of Bosnia and Herzegovina. Both homes of the BiH Parliamentary Assembly have proposed members of the task force, and the Council of Ministers has appointed a working group to monitor the situation and activities related to possible construction of a radioactive waste disposal site in Bulgaria. The working group is chaired by the minister of construction and spatial planning of Republika Srpska, Mrs. Srebrenka Golić, as a contact point for the Espoo Convention. The working group, although I must point out that the appointment was delayed, analysed in just two sessions the state of the procedure and the activities run by the Croatian National Institute for Radio and Nuclear Safety in order to establish a landfill of radioactive waste at the location of Trska Gora, and agreed with the consensus the text of the Proposal for a Resolution on the non-acceptation of the construction of the warehouse and disposal of nuclear waste used sources and spent nuclear fuel and the opposition to activities related to the construction of a warehouse and dump in the Trgovina Gora municipality, Dvor Municipality, the Republic of Croatia. As an MP and member of the Working Party from the House of Representatives, I call on my colleagues and colleagues to support the proposed Resolution for the official position of the BiH Parliamentary Assembly on this multiannual procedure. I would also like to point out that the delegates of the House of Peoples, who are members of the Working Group, will propose the same text to the House of Peoples of the Paraliamentary Assembly of BiH. Point 1. Resolutions are clearly established and we find that 91 the Parliamentary Assembly of BiH is most strongly opposed to the construction of warehouses and dumps of radioactive waste, used sources and spent nuclear fuel and activities related to the construction of the warehouse and dumps in the location of Trgovina Gora, Municipality of Dvor, Republic of Croatia. [[Employee is warned on time]] The resolution also requires the full engagement of all competent authorities of all levels of government in Bosnia and Herzegovina to challenge and prevent possible construction of the dump in question. As far as banking sector matters are concerned, we have inherited laws in the area, entity control is the work of banking agencies. At the level of the state, the Deposit Insurance Agency was formed, but unfortunately not the regulatory body that controls the quality of the banking system's assets throughout Bosnia and Herzegovina, and therefore we have this case, which we have witnessed backwards for several months, that the Deposit Insurance Agency actually had to pay off the insured deposits of up to 50,000 KM, without having been able to in any way affect the quality control of the assets or work of the banks themselves in the Bosnia and Herzegovina area. I think that this thing will change in the future, not in a way that we will take responsibility for anyone, but in a way that will increase coordination and control and mutual co-operation between banking agencies, the Deposit Insurance Agency and the Central Bank of BiH, which is in fact the regulatory body of the banking system in all neighbouring and other countries. So what is really important is the need for the whole of Bosnia and Herzegovina to have a regulated banking system and that we do not have such surprises as happened with Bobar Bank and Bank of Serbia, which significantly threaten the authority of the banking sector and our capacities in the area.
